**The joint designation register has allocated 526 numbers since August 1998, and its mission statements are built from three verbs.** Eighteen entries say demonstrate, five say investigate, and one says validate. Every one of those verbs takes a capability as its object. **Exactly one entry in the register uses the verb exhibit, and its object is a failure mode** [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]].

This is the fifty-seventh article in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54], and the [X-55][related_post_a352_lockheed_martin_x55].

**Every other aeroplane in this series was designed so that flutter would not happen, and spent a large part of its flight test budget proving that it did not.** This one was designed so that flutter would happen, inside the envelope rather than beyond it, and then spent its flight test budget flying into it on purpose and switching the suppression off to watch.

## The Registry Entry Is the Primary Document

**On 7 January 2012 the joint designation registry recorded the following against X-56A**, allocated to Lockheed Martin, powered by two Jetcat turbojets, and sponsored by the United States Air Force [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]].

> A remotely piloted, high aspect ratio, flying wing vehicle designed to exhibit multiple flutter mechanisms within its flight envelope.

The register is the public addendum to the Department of Defense instruction that governs how military aerospace vehicles are designated, and it is maintained alongside the allocation list proper [[DOD 4120.15-L, Model Designation of Military Aerospace Vehicles][ref_dod_412015l]] [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]].

Of the 526 allocation rows the addendum carries, this is **the only one containing the word flutter, the only one containing any form of the word exhibit, the only one naming a flying wing, the only one naming a high aspect ratio, and the only one naming a flight envelope**. Those five measurements are separate and they all return one, and they all return the same row.

**The uniqueness that matters is not any single one of them.** It is the verb. A register of experimental aircraft is a register of intentions, and the intentions it records are overwhelmingly to demonstrate that something works. **To exhibit a flutter mechanism is to undertake that something will fail**, in a controlled way, at a place of one's choosing.

**The claim has to be tested against its strongest counterexample rather than asserted**, because the two preceding articles in this series both opened with a uniqueness that measurement had to narrow. The counterexample here is real. The X-54A was to be `capable of generating relevant ground sonic boom signatures`, and the X-59A `will create a shaped sonic boom signature` [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]] [[X-Planes: Gulfstream X-54][related_post_a351_gulfstream_x54]]. **Both of those aeroplanes were also designed to produce an unwanted phenomenon on purpose.** Two entries mention a sonic boom, one mentions vortices, two mention icing, one mentions fatigue.

**The distinction that survives the counterexample is sharper than the one that did not.** A sonic boom is a nuisance on the ground and it does not threaten the aeroplane making it. Icing and fatigue are conditions an aeroplane is exposed to. **Flutter destroys the structure exhibiting it, and it does so in seconds.** The X-56A is the only entry in the register whose stated purpose is to produce a phenomenon capable of destroying the vehicle produced to demonstrate it.

## What Flutter Is, and Why It Is Not an Ordinary Hazard

**Flutter is not a vibration that gets worse.** It is a transfer of energy from the airstream into the structure, mediated by the phase between how a surface moves and how the air pushes back [[Aeroelasticity][ref_aeroelasticity]] [[Bisplinghoff, Ashley and Halfman, Aeroelasticity][book_bisplinghoff]].

**Flutter is one of two classical aeroelastic instabilities and it is the dynamic one** [[Flutter, Aeroelasticity][ref_flutter_wikipedia]]. The other is divergence, which is static, and which occurs when the aerodynamic moment twisting a wing exceeds the structural moment resisting it, at which point the twist runs away without any oscillation at all [[Aeroelasticity, Divergence][ref_divergence]] [[Dowell, A modern course in aeroelasticity][book_dowell]].

Two structural modes are usually enough. Each has a frequency and a shape, which is what a normal mode is [[Normal Mode][ref_normal_mode]], and each is damped by some fraction of the damping that would suppress oscillation entirely [[Damping Ratio][ref_damping_ratio]]. **Measuring those frequencies and dampings on a real structure is a discipline of its own** [[Ewins, Modal testing, theory and practice][book_ewins]], and the X-56 programme did it on the ground before it did it in the air. As airspeed rises the aerodynamic forces grow, the frequencies move, and at some speed two of them approach one another closely enough to couple. **Once they couple, the damping of one mode falls and the damping of the other rises**, and at the speed where the falling one reaches zero the structure will sustain an oscillation on the energy the airflow supplies. **Above that speed the damping is negative and the amplitude grows every cycle** [[Wright and Cooper, Introduction to aircraft aeroelasticity and loads][book_wright_cooper]] [[Hodges and Pierce, Introduction to structural dynamics and aeroelasticity][book_hodges_pierce]].

**The consequence is that flutter has no useful warning margin in the ordinary sense.** A structure at ninety-five percent of its flutter speed is not slightly fluttering. It is stable, and it will look stable, and the quantity that is about to go through zero is a damping ratio that no one aboard can feel. **This is why flutter is cleared by prediction and margin rather than by approaching it and observing**, and it is exactly the convention the X-56A was built to break.

The most widely known flutter event is not an aeroplane at all. The Tacoma Narrows bridge deck of 1940 is the canonical case, and bridge aerodynamics remains one of the two fields where the flutter boundary is a routine design quantity [[Tacoma Narrows Bridge, 1940][ref_tacoma_narrows]] [[Simiu and Scanlan, Wind effects on structures][book_simiu_scanlan]]. **That is not an aside**, and this article's reference survey is built on the observation that a bridge deck and a wing are the same problem with different boundary conditions.

## The Margin the Regulation Requires, Which This Aeroplane Deliberately Does Not Have

**A transport aeroplane is required to be free from flutter at every speed up to a margin above its design dive speed.** The airworthiness requirement is fifteen percent in equivalent airspeed above the design dive speed, and the design dive speed already sits above the maximum operating speed [[14 CFR 25.629, Aeroelastic Stability Requirements][ref_far_25_629]] [[14 CFR 25.335, Design Airspeeds][ref_far_25_335]]. **The X-56 programme's own summary of this convention is that aircraft typically carry around enough structure to keep flutter fifteen percent above the design dive speed** [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

$$
V_F \;\ge\; 1.15 \, V_D
$$

**The requirement is stated in equivalent airspeed and that is not a detail.** Flutter depends on dynamic pressure rather than on speed, and dynamic pressure is the quantity that equivalent airspeed is constructed to preserve.

$$
q = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho V^{2}
$$

$$
V_E = V \sqrt{\frac{\rho}{\rho_0}} = V \sqrt{\sigma}
$$

**Two aeroplanes at the same equivalent airspeed and different altitudes are at the same dynamic pressure and therefore at the same point relative to a flutter boundary**, while their true airspeeds differ by the square root of the density ratio [[Equivalent Airspeed][ref_equivalent_airspeed]] [[True Airspeed][ref_true_airspeed]] [[U.S. Standard Atmosphere][ref_us_standard_atmosphere]]. **Every speed quoted in this article is the speed its source quotes**, and the flight test report works in knots without stating a conversion, which is defensible for an aeroplane whose entire envelope sits within a few thousand feet of a dry lake bed.

**The phrase carry around enough structure is the entire economic argument.** Torsional and bending stiffness are bought with material, material is weight, and weight is fuel. **The flutter margin is therefore a mass penalty paid on every flight of every aeroplane in the fleet**, in exchange for the assurance that a boundary is never approached.

**The proposition the X-56 exists to test is that the margin could be supplied by a control system instead of by structure.** If a feedback law can add damping to the mode that is losing it, the boundary moves, and the structure does not have to be as stiff. **The wing may then be longer and thinner, which reduces induced drag, and lighter, which reduces everything.** The programme's stated goal was to increase aspect ratio by thirty to forty percent [[X-56A Multi-Utility Technology Testbed, NASA Facts FS 2015 08 105 AFRC][ref_x56_factsheet]].

**That is why the aeroplane had to flutter.** A control law that suppresses an instability cannot be validated against an aeroplane that never becomes unstable. **The demonstration requires the failure mode to be present, reachable, survivable and repeatable**, and no aeroplane certified under the ordinary rule offers any of those four.

## What Was Actually Built

**Two nearly identical aircraft were designed and built at Palmdale by the Lockheed Martin Skunk Works for the Air Force Research Laboratory** [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]] [[Skunk Works][ref_skunk_works]] [[Air Force Research Laboratory][ref_afrl]].

The vehicle is small. It has a wingspan of 28 feet and a length of about 7.5 feet, and its maximum gross takeoff weight is given as 525 pounds in the flight test report and 550 pounds in the programme retrospective, against about 480 pounds in the fact sheet [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]] [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]] [[X-56A Multi-Utility Technology Testbed, NASA Facts FS 2015 08 105 AFRC][ref_x56_factsheet]]. **Three weights from three documents of the same programme is worth stating rather than smoothing**, and the flight test report's figure is used throughout this article because it is the document that reports the flight results those weights condition.

Propulsion is two Jetcat P400 turbojets of about 90 pounds of thrust each [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]] [[JetCat, Model Turbojet Engines][ref_jetcat]]. **The register records the engine as the P-240 and the flight test report records it as the P400** [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]]. The register is a record of what was allocated in January 2012 and the report is a record of what flew, so the disagreement is unremarkable, but it is a disagreement and this article does not conceal it.

**The reason the engines are jets rather than propellers is a measurement decision.** A propeller produces low frequency excitation, and low frequency excitation is precisely the band in which the structural modes of interest live. **A test article built to measure the damping of a mode near two hertz cannot carry a machine that shakes it at two hertz.**

**The other half of the programme's purpose is gusts, which this article treats only in passing.** A flexible wing is loaded by atmospheric turbulence as well as by manoeuvre, the discipline of predicting those loads is old and well developed [[Hoblit, Gust loads on aircraft, concepts and applications][book_hoblit]], and gust load alleviation was named alongside flutter suppression in every statement of what the X-56 was for [[X-56A Multi-Utility Technology Testbed, NASA Facts FS 2015 08 105 AFRC][ref_x56_factsheet]].

**The aeroplane is modular and that is the whole design.** The system comprised two centre bodies, one set of stiff wings and three sets of flexible wings, along with a ground control station and a trailer [[X-56A Multi-Utility Technology Testbed, NASA Facts FS 2015 08 105 AFRC][ref_x56_factsheet]]. Every wing set shares the same outer mould line and differs only in structural properties [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**That is a controlled experiment built into the hardware.** The stiff wings put flutter outside the achievable envelope and were used to collect rigid body aerodynamic and control data and to prove out the aircraft systems. The flexible wings put at least one flutter mode well inside it. **The aerodynamics is held constant and the structure is the variable**, which is the only way to attribute a change in behaviour to flexibility rather than to shape.

There are ten trailing edge control surfaces, two body flaps and four wing flaps on each side, along with a three axis gyro set, an inertial unit with satellite navigation, and ten vertical accelerometers distributed through the wings and centre body [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]]. One set of flexible wings additionally carried fibre optic strain sensing [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**And the aeroplane carried a ballistic parachute, intended to recover the fuselage and most of the aircraft systems in the event of an in-flight wing failure** [[X-56A Multi-Utility Technology Testbed, NASA Facts FS 2015 08 105 AFRC][ref_x56_factsheet]] [[Ballistic Recovery Systems][ref_brs]]. **A recovery system sized for losing a wing is a design document in itself.** It says what the programme thought might happen.

## Body Freedom Flutter, Which Is What a Tail Would Have Prevented

**The specific instability this aeroplane was built to produce is body freedom flutter**, and understanding why the configuration was chosen is understanding the whole vehicle [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

**A conventional aeroplane has a tail, and the tail sets the frequency of its short period pitch mode.** The short period is a rigid body oscillation in angle of attack and pitch rate, and on a tailed aeroplane its frequency is high because the tail is a long way from the centre of gravity and generates a large restoring moment [[Etkin and Reid, Dynamics of flight][book_etkin]] [[Phugoid][ref_phugoid]] [[Short Period, Longitudinal Modes][ref_short_period]] [[Nelson, Flight stability and automatic control][book_nelson]] [[Stengel, Flight dynamics][book_stengel]]. **The first wing bending mode is an elastic oscillation and its frequency is set by the stiffness and mass distribution of the wing.** On a conventional aeroplane these two frequencies are far apart, and modes that are far apart in frequency do not exchange energy.

**A tailless flying wing has neither of those separations** [[Flying Wing][ref_flying_wing]]. Its pitch inertia is low because there is no tail mass at a long arm, and its aspect ratio is high because that is the point of it, so the first wing bending frequency is low.

$$
AR = \frac{b^{2}}{S}
$$

**Aspect ratio is the quantity the whole programme is trying to increase** [[Aspect Ratio, Aeronautics][ref_aspect_ratio]], and it enters the problem twice. It reduces induced drag, which is why anyone wants it. **And it lowers the bending frequency of the wing, which is what brings that frequency down into the band where the rigid body modes live.** The benefit and the hazard are the same geometric change. **The rigid body mode and the elastic mode end up in the same frequency band**, and the flight test report states the mechanism plainly, which is that the low pitch inertia and high aspect ratio of flying wings tends to cause rigid body flight modes and flexible modes to occur within the same frequency bands and to interact.

The coupling itself has a clean physical description. **As the centre body pitches up, the wings bend symmetrically upward**, and that bending is in a phase relationship with the pitching that allows energy to pass between them [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]]. As airspeed increases the short period frequency rises and the first symmetric wing bending frequency falls slightly. **By about 100 knots the two are close enough to couple.** One pole moves left and becomes more stable, the other moves right, and at the speed where the second one crosses the imaginary axis the aeroplane is fluttering. **The picture is a root locus in which the parameter being varied is not a control gain but the dynamic pressure** [[Root Locus Analysis][ref_root_locus]].

**This is the sense in which the aeroplane is not merely flexible but is a different kind of dynamic system.** The flight dynamics and the structural dynamics are not two problems that can be solved separately and checked against one another. **They are one problem**, and the model the programme built reflects that. It carries 325 states, being 12 rigid body states, displacement and velocity for each of the first 25 structural modes, 93 aerodynamic lag states, 30 actuator states, 6 engine states and 134 sensor states [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]] [[State-Space Representation][ref_state_space]].

$$
n_s = 12 + 2\times 25 + 93 + 30 + 6 + 134 = 325
$$

**Those parts sum to exactly 325**, which is worth checking rather than repeating, because a decomposition that does not add up is a misread decomposition. **The largest single block is not the structure and not the aerodynamics.** It is the 134 sensor states, which is more than the rigid body, structural and actuator states combined.

## The Wreck Behind the Programme

**A flying wing of very high aspect ratio had already been lost, and the loss is the reason this work was funded.**

The Helios Prototype was a solar powered flying wing built to fly very high for a very long time, and the last of a line that had begun with Pathfinder [[NASA Pathfinder][ref_pathfinder]]. On 26 June 2003, flying from the Pacific Missile Range Facility on Kauai, it encountered turbulence of a kind the crew considered ordinary. **The disturbance produced an abnormally high wing dihedral**, the aircraft entered an undamped pitch oscillation, the airspeed excursions grew with every cycle until the design speed was exceeded, and the resulting dynamic pressure tore the leading edge secondary structure and the upper surface skin off the outer panels [[NASA Releases Helios Prototype Aircraft Mishap Report][ref_helios_mishap]] [[NASA Helios Prototype][ref_helios_wikipedia]].

**The mishap board's finding is the sentence that matters here.** The mishap resulted from the inability to predict, using available analysis methods, the aircraft's increased sensitivity to atmospheric disturbances following configuration changes made for the long duration demonstration [[NASA Releases Helios Prototype Aircraft Mishap Report][ref_helios_mishap]].

**That is not a finding about a wing. It is a finding about a model.** The aeroplane behaved in a way the available methods could not anticipate, in a flight condition nobody thought was extreme. **A programme whose response to that finding is to build a model is a programme that has understood it**, and a model of a highly coupled flexible aeroplane cannot be validated against an aeroplane that does not exhibit the coupling. The X-56A is the answer to Helios, and the register's verb is the answer stated in one word.

## Finding a Boundary You Intend to Cross

**The problem of approaching a flutter boundary is that the quantity you need is the one that has not happened yet.** Damping is estimated from how a structure responds to a disturbance, and the estimate is only meaningful at the speed where it was taken. **Extrapolating a trend in damping toward zero is the oldest method and it is unreliable**, because the trend is not linear and the last part of it is the part that matters.

The method the programme used instead is due to Zimmerman and Weissenburger and dates from 1964. **It forms a single stability parameter out of the frequencies and damping of the two modes involved in the mechanism**, fits a second order curve to that parameter against airspeed, and takes the crossing of the axis as the predicted flutter speed [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

$$
F = \left[\left(\frac{\omega_2^{2}-\omega_1^{2}}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{\beta_2^{2}-\beta_1^{2}}{2}\right)\right]^{2}+ 4\,\beta_1\beta_2\left[\left(\frac{\omega_2^{2}+\omega_1^{2}}{2}\right)+ 2\left(\frac{\beta_1^{2}+\beta_2^{2}}{2}\right)\right]- \left(\frac{\beta_2-\beta_1}{\beta_1+\beta_2}\right)\left[\left(\frac{\omega_2^{2}-\omega_1^{2}}{2}\right)+ 2\left(\frac{\beta_1^{2}+\beta_2^{2}}{2}\right)\right]
$$

**The parameter is defined only below the boundary**, which is the property that makes it useful and also the property that makes it awkward. It gives an estimate of a speed the aeroplane has not reached from data taken where the aeroplane is safe, and it stops being defined at exactly the moment the answer would be observable.

**Its accuracy improved as the boundary was approached, which is both reassuring and the reason the buildup had to be incremental.** With data only from low speed, far from flutter, the prediction was significantly wrong because the extrapolation was long. **From ten knots away it was within one knot**, which on a boundary of 111 knots is under one percent [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

**Alongside the damping estimates the programme recovered a dynamic model from measured inputs and outputs at every cleared condition, which is system identification** [[System Identification][ref_system_identification]], performed in the air on a vehicle whose model was the thing in question. The structure underneath it is an ordinary aircraft structure in every respect except how much it bends [[Megson, Aircraft structures for engineering students][book_megson]].

The envelope was expanded in ten knot increments until the predicted boundary was within ten knots, and in five knot increments after that. At each new condition the aeroplane was excited with a short doublet, called a rap, applied to the outboard surfaces, of 0.25 seconds and one to one and a half degrees of amplitude.

$$
\zeta_{\text{closed loop}} > 0.04
$$

**The requirement to proceed was a closed loop damping ratio above 0.04**, and separately a gain margin above 3 decibels and a phase margin above 30 degrees [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]] [[Gain Margin][ref_gain_margin]] [[Phase Margin][ref_phase_margin]].

## The Two Stability Problems Disagree About Which Fuel State Is Dangerous

**The fuel tank is in the tail, and this produces a result worth stating carefully.**

As fuel burns, the centre of gravity moves forward, and a forward centre of gravity is more statically stable in pitch. **The report therefore records the greatest instability condition as occurring at takeoff**, with static pitch stability increasing as fuel is consumed [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

**The flutter boundary runs the other way.** At the low fuel and forward centre of gravity condition, flutter onset was found at about 111 knots. At the high fuel and aft centre of gravity condition it was about 114 knots.

**So the fuel state that is statically the safer one is the aeroelastically more dangerous one.** The aeroplane is most statically unstable when it is full and least statically unstable when it is nearly empty, and it flutters earliest when it is nearly empty. **Three knots is not a large number, but the sign is the finding**, and the sign is the opposite of what a single intuition about stability would produce.

**This is a specific instance of a general fact that the whole programme is about.** Static stability and aeroelastic stability are different properties of the same aeroplane, they depend on the mass distribution in different ways, and improving one can degrade the other. **An aeroplane with two stability problems that disagree has to be cleared against both**, and neither clearance is evidence about the other.

## Flying Past It, and Switching the Controller Off to Watch

**The programme flew the aeroplane above its open loop flutter speed under active control, and this is the result the whole vehicle exists to produce** [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

The flight campaign at the Armstrong Flight Research Center ran to 31 flights and expanded the airspeed from 60 to 120 knots, against a flutter onset between 111 and 114 knots [[Armstrong Flight Research Center][ref_armstrong]] [[X-56A Multi-Utility Technology Testbed, NASA][ref_nasa_x56]]. The flying was done over Edwards Air Force Base and Rogers Dry Lake, which has been the ground under most of the aeroplanes in this series [[Edwards Air Force Base][ref_edwards]] [[Rogers Dry Lake][ref_rogers_dry_lake]]. The programme retrospective describes the achievement as flying up to about ten percent past the open loop flutter instability [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**Then they turned the control system off in flight.**

The procedure is worth stating in full because it is the most direct realisation of the register's verb that any of these aeroplanes has produced. **A rap was applied to the outboard surfaces to excite the flutter mode with a repeatable amplitude larger than background turbulence.** Immediately after the rap, every control surface command was frozen at its current value and held, so that the uncontrolled dynamics of the aeroplane could be observed. **The duration was configurable in flight and was built up from one period of the flutter mode to three or four oscillations**, so that the damping could be measured. Then the control system was re-engaged and damped the motion out [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

**Below the boundary this showed low but positive damping. At the boundary it showed neutral stability. Above the boundary the damping was negative and the oscillation grew.** About two knots past onset the instability grew strongly enough that by the third open loop oscillation the dynamics were approaching test limits, and the airspeed progression for these particular test points was stopped.

**Two knots.** The margin between an aeroplane that can be left uncontrolled for three cycles and one that cannot is two knots out of 111.

## The Limit Cycle the Suppressor Could Not See

**One of the most distinctive signs of being near the boundary was a small limit cycle oscillation in pitch, and its cause is a mechanism worth separating from the flutter itself.**

The limit cycle was produced by deadbands in the actuators [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]] [[Deadband][ref_deadband]] [[Limit Cycle][ref_limit_cycle]]. An actuator with a deadband does not respond to a command below some threshold. **While the motion was inside that threshold the aeroplane was effectively open loop, and therefore unstable**, so the amplitude grew. Once the amplitude was large enough to push the actuators out of the deadband, the control system regained effectiveness and arrested the growth.

**The result is an aeroplane that is unstable in the small and stable in the large**, which is the inverse of the usual arrangement and a consequence of the suppressor being unable to see what it was supposed to suppress. **The instability was bounded by the nonlinearity that caused it.**

## The Aeroplane Built to Flutter Was Destroyed by Rotation

**The first X-56A was lost on 19 November 2015 on its first takeoff with flexible wings, and it was not lost to flutter** [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]] [[Lockheed Martin X-56 MUTT, Directory of U.S. Military Rockets and Missiles][ref_x56_dsn]].

The aircraft, operated by Lockheed Martin, had already flown a successful campaign with the stiff wings. **Immediately after rotation it pitched up, stalled, and crashed back onto the ground, a complete loss of the vehicle.**

The mechanism has two parts and both are consequences of the design rather than accidents.

**The first is that the aeroplane is statically unstable.** On a statically stable aeroplane the aerodynamic centre is aft of the centre of gravity, so during rotation the lift force arrives nearly collocated with the main gear and produces little moment change. **On a statically unstable aeroplane the lift arrives well forward of the main gear**, and the result is a positive pitch acceleration exactly when the pilot is already commanding a pitch up.

**The second is the flexibility itself.** During rotation the flexible wings bend upward as lift builds. That bending gives the wingtip a vertical velocity, vertical motion into the oncoming airflow reduces the local angle of attack, and reduced angle of attack reduces the tip lift. **On an aft swept wing, losing lift at the tip is a pitch up moment.** The programme estimated from wing video that this additional moment was likely enough to push the vehicle into stall.

**So the aeroplane commissioned to exhibit an aeroelastic instability was destroyed by an aeroelastic effect, at about sixty knots, with the flutter boundary fifty knots away and entirely irrelevant.** The effect that killed it is not flutter. It is a quasi-static coupling between wing bending and pitching moment, operating during the few seconds of rotation, in a regime the modelling effort had not been aimed at.

**The programme's own account of why is unusually direct.** At the time of the mishaps, a piloted simulation with fully coupled structural dynamics was an active area of research for the team but was not a requirement for airworthiness. The fully coupled models that existed were built for the up and away condition and were not useful for a vehicle transitioning to or from the ground, and did not include landing gear dynamics [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**The model was excellent where the research was and absent where the flying was.**

## Landing Was the Other Dangerous Part

**The same coupling threatened the other end of the flight, and this time it was caught before it cost an aeroplane.**

Ground drop tests conducted before the NASA flexible wing flights showed a significant reduction in nose bounce damping compared with the stiff wing and centre body only drops. **A large amount of energy went into the wings rather than the landing gear, the wing structure is very poorly damped compared with the gear, and wing bending couples into pitch** [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]]. The estimate was that the reduction in damping from stiff to flexible wings would likely produce an instability on landing.

The fixes were partly geometric and partly control. **The gear geometry was adjusted so the aeroplane sat at a higher angle of attack on the ground**, so that some lift was already present and less rotation was required, on the same principle that gives a B-52 its ground attitude. The main gear was moved forward, taking the tip back angle from about eighteen degrees to about ten. **And a controller was designed to actively damp the structural dynamics during landing.**

**The first flexible wing flight at Armstrong was on 31 August 2017, and the actual takeoff and landing dynamics matched the fully coupled nonlinear simulation very well** [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**The programme's summary of the risk allocation is the sentence that ought to be quoted whenever a testbed is designed.** Takeoff and landing were more risky than flutter testing, in part because the landing gear design was constrained by the parachute landing case rather than being optimised for normal landings. **You want your risk in the research under test, not in the normal operations of the testbed.**

## The Register Said Mechanisms, and the Aeroplane Exhibited One

**The register's noun is plural and this is the one claim in the entry that the flight programme did not deliver.**

The first mechanism, body freedom flutter, was found between 111 and 114 knots depending on fuel state. **A second mechanism was predicted from the flight data, involving the first symmetric wing bending mode and the first symmetric wing torsion mode**, at 138 knots at low fuel and 144 knots at high fuel [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]].

**The maximum level flight speed of the aeroplane is 135 knots.** It can reach 150 knots in a dive. **So the second mechanism sits between three and nine knots above anything the aeroplane can do in level flight**, and is reachable only in a descent.

**And the envelope was expanded to 120 knots, which is eighteen knots short of the second mechanism at its most optimistic.** The aeroplane never exhibited it.

$$
\frac{q_2}{q_1} = \left(\frac{V_2}{V_1}\right)^{2}= \left(\frac{138}{111}\right)^{2} = 1.55
$$

**The gap is larger than it looks, because flutter is a dynamic pressure before it is a speed.** Reaching 138 knots from 111 requires 55 percent more dynamic pressure, not the 24 percent more airspeed that the two numbers suggest [[Dynamic Pressure][ref_dynamic_pressure]].

**The reason the second mechanism was not reached is recorded, and it is not aerodynamic.** The programme's retrospective states that the vehicle was capable of likely flying deeper into flutter and possibly getting close to the second flutter mode, and that with only one vehicle left the team became too risk averse, and that the Air Force Research Laboratory kept the project going just enough to get past flutter [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**The register's plural was defeated by arithmetic about spare airframes.** The retrospective's own recommendation is to build multiple vehicles at the beginning and plan to lose one or two, on the grounds that at this scale the vehicles cost little against engineering labour over the life of the programme, and that a single remaining vehicle forces a risk posture that prevents the research it was built for. **Three vehicles, it concludes, would likely have been the sweet spot.**

**This is a precise and unusual finding.** The entry in the register was not aspirational in the way an unbuilt aeroplane's entry is aspirational. The aeroplane was built, it flew, it fluttered, it was flown past its boundary under control, and the suppression was demonstrated by switching it off and on again. **Every clause of the mission statement was delivered except the plural**, and the plural failed for want of a spare airframe rather than for want of a mechanism.

## The Second Entry, and the Question Mark That Belongs to the Register

**There are two X-56 rows and the second one is strange.**

The X-56B is recorded against Lockheed Martin with NASA as sponsor and a mission statement of seven words, being a derivative of X-56A with a new wing. **Its date is given as 2019 followed by a question mark, and the question mark is the register's own** [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]].

**That is worth measuring rather than passing over, because this register is otherwise precise to the day.** Of the 526 allocation rows, seven carry a question mark in the date field. **The X-56B is the only X designation among them.**

The register also marks certain rows as not drawn from Department of Defense records. **Eleven rows in the whole register carry that mark, and of the thirty X designation rows the X-56B is the only one** [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]]. The company it keeps in that set is instructive. It consists of Stinger and Javelin variants, two training and test versions of a naval missile, a long range air to air missile, the Blackjack unmanned aircraft, and the RQ-170 Sentinel.

**The common property of that list is that the designation is known but the allocation record is not.** For the RQ-170 the reason is classification. For the missile variants the reason is that a spiral upgrade is often fielded before its designation appears in a public list.

**For the X-56B the reason appears to be that the vehicle was reconfigured rather than allocated.** The second airframe was reactivated in an improved configuration and flew again, and secondary sources place that first flight on 19 April 2021 and a crash shortly after takeoff on 9 July 2021 [[Lockheed Martin X-56 MUTT, Directory of U.S. Military Rockets and Missiles][ref_x56_dsn]] [[Lockheed Martin X-56][ref_x56_wikipedia]]. **Those dates are two years after the date the register records with a question mark**, and this article does not resolve the discrepancy. It records that the register itself declines to assert the date, and that this is the only X designation about which it does so.

**A register that marks its own uncertainty is a better document than one that does not.** The question mark is not a defect in the source. It is the source telling a reader which of its cells to trust less, and in fifty-seven articles of reading this register that is the first time it has done so about an X designation.

## What the Aeroplane Cost and What It Bought

**Fifty-four flights were flown across the two vehicles and all wing configurations** [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]]. Thirty-one of those were the flexible wing campaign at Armstrong between 2017 and 2019 [[Schaefer and others, Flying Beyond Flutter with the X-56A Aircraft, NASA TM 20220012337][ref_beyond_flutter]]. **One vehicle was destroyed on the first flexible wing takeoff in 2015 and the surviving vehicle was lost in 2021 in its X-56B configuration.**

**What the programme bought is a set of flight measurements of a coupled flexible aeroplane past its own stability boundary**, which did not exist before and cannot be produced in a wind tunnel, because a wind tunnel model is not free in pitch and body freedom flutter requires the pitch freedom.

**What it also bought is a well documented disagreement between models and flight.** The retrospective records that many areas were found where the high fidelity up and away linear models did not match flight data, that control laws had to be retuned several times, that entire control surfaces were omitted from the laws because of poor matching, and that some of the differences are still not understood [[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned, Aerospace Control and Guidance Systems Committee Meeting 127][ref_x56_lessons]].

**That last admission is the most valuable sentence in the programme's output.** A flight programme that reports agreement teaches nothing that the model did not already contain. **A flight programme that reports a disagreement it cannot yet explain, and keeps the data, has produced the only thing a testbed can produce that an analysis cannot.**

## The Source Base

**The survey behind this article rests on 3,862 curated records.** Four sweeps of Crossref, the NASA Technical Reports Server and the Defense Technical Information Center returned 6,477 records. A homonym store removed 485 of them, being 7.5 percent, and a subject gate kept 3,971 of the remainder. Deduplication removed a further 108.

### The Store Was Armed Against a Word This Article Cannot Do Without

**The word flutter belongs to cardiology as much as to aeroelasticity**, and the sweep store knows it. Of the 485 records the store removed, 26 were atrial flutter, including a paper titled simply on flutter and fibrillation and several on catheter ablation of the cavotricuspid isthmus. **That family stays armed and it is the purest homonym the store holds.**

**Four families were opened, and the decision was taken against records rather than against family names.** Together they returned 103 records to the pool.

| Family | Records | Why it is this article's subject |
|---|---:|---|
| Wind energy | 81 | A turbine blade is a flexible lifting surface that flutters, and the literature includes active suppression of exactly that |
| Civil structures | 14 | Bridge deck flutter is identified with the flutter margin method, which is the instrument this aeroplane used on itself |
| Fracture | 8 | A suppression law changes the load spectrum, and two of these papers are on the leverage of flight control over crack growth in a flexible aircraft |
| Smart actuators | 0 | Split out of a broader entry by this article, because the tag was narrower than the word it carried |

### Primary Documents

**275 of the 3,862 curated records are technical reports rather than journal or conference literature**, being 7.1 percent. That measure counts an address at the NASA Technical Reports Server or a Defense Technical Information Center identifier. **It is a measure of where a document is hosted and not of what it is**, so it undercounts, and the two documents this article leans on hardest are both inside it.

### The Age of the Literature

**The median year of the curated records is 2014.** 1,776 of them, being 48 percent, were published in 2015 or later, and 236 predate 1980. **A survey of an instability first understood in the 1930s that is three quarters contemporary is telling you that the control problem is the live one**, because the aerodynamics has not changed and the actuators and the computers have.

### What the Gate Was Built For, and What It Refuses

**The gate is A350's gate reordered.** That article was about a wing twisted on purpose, and its own comment on its flutter block called flutter the dynamic problem the flight programme deliberately dropped [[X-Planes: Boeing X-53 Active Aeroelastic Wing][related_post_a350_boeing_x53]]. **Here the dropped problem is the keystone**, and the block that stood as a boundary term there is promoted to the front. Nothing from the X-55 article survives, because a shared contractor is not a shared subject [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-55 ACCA][related_post_a352_lockheed_martin_x55]].

**It refuses hypersonics deliberately.** Panel flutter under piston theory is a real instability and a different regime from a 120 knot flying wing, and this series treats it elsewhere. It refuses missiles, marine vehicles, surface transport and turbomachinery for the same reason, which is that each has a flutter literature of its own that would swamp this one.

**The pool divides into 15 clusters and the largest is not the aeroplane.** It is the flutter literature itself.

| Cluster | Records |
|---|---:|
| Body freedom flutter, which is the mechanism the configuration was chosen to produce | 54 |
| Suppressing it, which is what the programme actually became | 317 |
| Finding the boundary before you reach it | 129 |
| Flutter itself, and the boundary an aeroplane is cleared against | 659 |
| Wings flexible enough that the equations change | 231 |
| Unsteady aerodynamics, because a steady theory cannot predict an oscillation | 347 |
| Control laws for an aircraft that will not hold its shape | 302 |
| Load alleviation, which is the other thing a flexible wing can be asked to do | 129 |
| Shaping and scaling a structure so that it flutters where you want it to | 26 |
| Measuring a structure, on the ground and in the air | 303 |
| The configuration, and the class of aeroplane this belongs to | 565 |
| Flight dynamics, and the modes a tail would have separated | 35 |
| The flow over the wing at these speeds | 54 |
| The atmosphere and the flight condition | 49 |
| Other aeronautical, structural and control literature | 662 |
| **Total** | **3,862** |

## What the Data Changed

**The opening claimed too much and the register narrowed it.** The first draft of this article asserted that the X-56A is the only entry in the register whose purpose is to produce an unwanted phenomenon. **It is not.** Two entries name a sonic boom, one names vortices, two name icing and one names fatigue. The claim that survives measurement is narrower and better, being that it is the only entry whose stated purpose is to produce a phenomenon capable of destroying the vehicle built to demonstrate it.

**The gate and the reference list were reading different strings, and only one of them was clean.** Forty-five harvested titles carry HTML markup or entities. Every reference definition in this corpus passes through a cleaner that decodes and strips them, which is why no published title carries a tag. **Nothing was cleaning the title the gate reads.** A paper on fixed order robust control was matched against a pattern for the term it is about, written in markup the pattern cannot see, and was refused as off subject. **A defect that only ever deletes evidence leaves no trace in the output**, and cleaning at load recovered records and cost nothing.

**Cluster assignment is first match wins, so the order of the list is a measurement decision and not a presentation choice.** A general pattern for flutter placed above a specific pattern for flutter suppression reported the programme's own subject at 19 records. **Moving the specific cluster above the general one it is a special case of reported 325.** The survey understated the article's central topic by a factor of seventeen, and the cause was the order of two entries in a list.

**A tag in the sweep store was broader than its own name.** The entry was the bare word dielectric, tagged as cure monitoring. Dielectric cure monitoring is a real subject and the tag was correct for the article that added it. **But a dielectric barrier discharge is a plasma actuator and a dielectric elastomer is a soft actuator**, and both are actuation technologies a flutter suppression survey wants. Opening the tag to reach them would also have readmitted the binary liquid mixtures the pattern was written for. **The entry was split so that each half can be opened alone.**

**A test asserted the wrong thing about the store and the suite caught it.** The first version of the new test claimed that opening the cure monitoring tag would readmit the binary liquid records. It does not, because a second and untagged pattern also covers them. **The store defends that incident twice**, which is a property worth pinning rather than a failure, and it was found because a new pattern was given a test in the same change.

## Epistemic State

**Facts.** The register's wording, the counts of terms across its 526 allocation rows, the question mark and the not from Department of Defense records marking on the X-56B row, and every number attributed above to the flight test report or the programme retrospective, are quoted from those documents and each numeric relation stated here has been recomputed rather than repeated.

**Inference.** That the register's verb marks a category difference rather than a wording accident is an inference from the distribution of verbs across the register, and it is supported by counting rather than proved by it. That the second flutter mechanism was not reached for programmatic rather than physical reasons is an inference, although an unusually well supported one, because the programme states it in those terms itself.

**Hypothesis.** That the X-56B row is marked as outside Department of Defense records because the vehicle was reconfigured rather than newly allocated is a hypothesis. It is consistent with the seven word mission statement and with the two year gap between the register's questioned date and the dates secondary sources give for the flights, but this article has not found a document that states it.

**Unresolved.** The disagreement between the register's P-240 engine and the flight test report's P400 is recorded and not explained. The three different maximum weights given by three documents of the same programme are recorded and not reconciled. The programme's own statement that some model to flight discrepancies remain not understood is quoted as it stands.

## Out of Scope

**The certification consequences of active flutter suppression are out of scope**, although they are the reason the work matters commercially, because they belong to airworthiness policy rather than to aeroelasticity and this article's gate refuses them by design.

**Bridge and wind turbine flutter are in the reference survey but not in the argument.** They are the same physics and the survey is built to include them, but the X-56 is an aeroplane and the article stays with the aeroplane.

## Conclusion

**The register records intentions, and its ordinary verb is demonstrate.** Eighteen entries demonstrate a capability, five investigate one, one validates a manufacturing method. **One entry undertakes to exhibit a failure mode**, and that entry belongs to a 525 pound remotely piloted flying wing with a 28 foot span and two model turbojets.

**The undertaking was met in every particular but one.** The aeroplane was built with one stiff wing set and three flexible ones sharing an outer mould line, so that structure was the only variable. It flew to a boundary predicted from subcritical data by a method from 1964, and the prediction was within one knot from ten knots away. It flew past that boundary under active control. **And on several occasions the control system was frozen in flight, after a deliberate excitation, so that the instability could be observed for three or four oscillations before the suppression was restored.**

**The one clause not delivered is the plural.** A second mechanism was predicted from the flight data at 138 knots, three knots above the maximum level flight speed and reachable only in a dive, and the programme stopped eighteen knots short of it. **It stopped there because the first aeroplane had been destroyed, and one remaining aeroplane cannot be risked the way two can.**

**And the first aeroplane was not destroyed by flutter.** It was destroyed during rotation at about sixty knots, when its flexible wings bent up as lift arrived, the tips lost angle of attack, the aft swept planform turned that into a pitch up moment, and the aeroplane stalled off a takeoff it had made sixteen times before with stiffer wings. **The instability the programme was funded to study killed it in a form the programme had not modelled, in the one part of the flight nobody had thought was the research.**

**That is the lesson the programme itself draws, and it generalises past this aeroplane.** The models were built where the interesting physics was and were absent where the ordinary flying was. **A testbed built entirely around its experiment will find that its risk is concentrated in everything else.**
